People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… not as much. I mean, I usually really love painting—like I’ll get all my brushes out and lose a whole afternoon without even noticing. And I like going to museum exhibits too, just kind of wandering and looking at things, even the little description plaques, which my family makes fun of me for reading every word.

But lately my paints are just sitting there. I see them and think, “I should,” and then I just… don’t. And there was an exhibit I would normally be excited about, but I couldn’t make myself care enough to go. It feels weird, because those are very “me” things.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F43.23, G47.73\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is I’m just really tired. Like, exhausted in a way that sleeping doesn’t seem to fix. I keep thinking maybe it’s just work, because being a manager is always a lot—meetings, emails, people needing things, and then I get home and there’s still dinner and normal life stuff. But lately even a regular workday and just getting through dinner at home feels heavier than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, such as hobbies or spending time with friends and family, as much as you did before feeling this exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not as much. I mean, I usually really love painting—like I’ll get all my brushes out and lose a whole afternoon without even noticing. And I like going to museum exhibits too, just kind of wandering and looking at things, even the little description plaques, which my family makes fun of me for reading every word.\n\nBut lately my paints are just sitting there. Yeah… I think so. Most days lately I wake up with this kind of heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it’s even started. It’s not always dramatic crying or anything—sometimes I still get up and do what I’m supposed to do—but it’s just there.

Not really… I keep trying to think of one, but the things I usually look forward to just don’t land the same. But lately even a regular workday and just getting through dinner at home feels heavier than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, such as hobbies or spending time with friends and family, as much as you did before feeling this exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not as much. I mean, I usually really love painting—like I’ll get all my brushes out and lose a whole afternoon without even noticing. And I like going to museum exhibits too, just kind of wandering and looking at things, even the little description plaques, which my family makes fun of me for reading every word.\n\nBut lately my paints are just sitting there. I see them and think, “I should,” and then I just… don’t. And there was an exhibit I would normally be excited about, but I couldn’t make myself care enough to go. It feels weird, because those are very “me” things.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since you started noticing this exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days lately I wake up with this kind of heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it’s even started. It’s not always dramatic crying or anything—sometimes I still get up and do what I’m supposed to do—but it’s just there.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be more of a morning person, or at least I’d make coffee and look at the light in the kitchen and think about what needed doing. Now I’ll stand there and notice the dishes or some random thing on the counter and it all just feels… flat. Like the whole day is already weighted down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It’s a little hard to pin down because at first I was just sort of unusually energized and really driven—like I was pushing through things and taking on more—and then it shifted into this low, heavy feeling that’s been sticking around. But the whole swing, from that revved-up phase to now feeling persistently down, has been around a month.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really… I keep trying to think of one, but the things I usually look forward to just don’t land the same. Painting is the big one—I normally love setting everything out, mixing colors, making a mess of the table. Lately the supplies are just sitting there, and I keep walking past them like, “Maybe later,” and then later never happens.\n\nAnd museums, too. There was this exhibit I probably would’ve dragged someone to before—or gone by myself, honestly, because I like taking my time. But I just couldn’t make myself care enough to go. It’s strange, because those are usually the things that make me feel like myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean… not completely. I’m still going to work, still getting the basic stuff done at home. It’s not like everything has fallen apart.

But I am slower making decisions at work, which is awkward because, you know, I’m supposed to be the person people come to for decisions. And at home I’ve been less dependable with plans—like I’ll say we should do something as a family and then I kind of fade out of it or forget to follow through.

And I haven’t really been doing my art things much lately, which is… that’s usually how I stay connected with people. I keep meaning to go, and then I just don’t.

I mean, I usually really love painting—like I’ll get all my brushes out and lose a whole afternoon without even noticing. Most days lately I wake up with this kind of heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it’s even started. It’s not always dramatic crying or anything—sometimes I still get up and do what I’m supposed to do—but it’s just there.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be more of a morning person, or at least I’d make coffee and look at the light in the kitchen and think about what needed doing. Now I’ll stand there and notice the dishes or some random thing on the counter and it all just feels… flat. Like the whole day is already weighted down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It’s a little hard to pin down because at first I was just sort of unusually energized and really driven—like I was pushing through things and taking on more—and then it shifted into this low, heavy feeling that’s been sticking around. But the whole swing, from that revved-up phase to now feeling persistently down, has been around a month.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really… I keep trying to think of one, but the things I usually look forward to just don’t land the same. Painting is the big one—I normally love setting everything out, mixing colors, making a mess of the table. Lately the supplies are just sitting there, and I keep walking past them like, “Maybe later,” and then later never happens.\n\nAnd museums, too. I keep meaning to go, and then I just don’t.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically since you started feeling this way?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 46 / manager / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Psychomotor_Changes\nmanifestation: \"My staff has noticed I’m slower to answer questions in meetings, like there’s a pause before I can get my thoughts and words moving.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Kind of, yes. Not like I’m physically dragging my feet around the house exactly, but at work people have noticed I’m… slower, I guess.

In meetings, someone will ask me something, and there’s this pause before I can get the thought together and actually say it. One of my staff sort of joked about it—well, not joked meanly, just noticed—and then I started noticing it too. Like my words are stuck in traffic.

It’s embarrassing, because meetings already have enough weird pauses, and then everyone starts looking at their notebooks or pretending to check the agenda. But yes, I think I am slower to respond than usual.

But lately even a regular workday and just getting through dinner at home feels heavier than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, such as hobbies or spending time with friends and family, as much as you did before feeling this exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not as much. I mean, I usually really love painting—like I’ll get all my brushes out and lose a whole afternoon without even noticing. I see them and think, “I should,” and then I just… don’t. And there was an exhibit I would normally be excited about, but I couldn’t make myself care enough to go. It feels weird, because those are very “me” things.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since you started noticing this exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days lately I wake up with this kind of heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it’s even started. It’s not always dramatic crying or anything—sometimes I still get up and do what I’m supposed to do—but it’s just there.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be more of a morning person, or at least I’d make coffee and look at the light in the kitchen and think about what needed doing. Now I’ll stand there and notice the dishes or some random thing on the counter and it all just feels… flat. Like the whole day is already weighted down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It’s a little hard to pin down because at first I was just sort of unusually energized and really driven—like I was pushing through things and taking on more—and then it shifted into this low, heavy feeling that’s been sticking around. But the whole swing, from that revved-up phase to now feeling persistently down, has been around a month.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really… I keep trying to think of one, but the things I usually look forward to just don’t land the same. Painting is the big one—I normally love setting everything out, mixing colors, making a mess of the table. Lately the supplies are just sitting there, and I keep walking past them like, “Maybe later,” and then later never happens.\n\nAnd museums, too. There was this exhibit I probably would’ve dragged someone to before—or gone by myself, honestly, because I like taking my time. But I just couldn’t make myself care enough to go. It’s strange, because those are usually the things that make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in almost all activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, “almost all” sounds so big, but… the things I usually really love, like painting, I just don’t touch now. Lately I can’t make myself care enough to go, which is odd because normally that’s the kind of thing I’d plan my whole afternoon around.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of elevated mood or increased energy during the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah… earlier in the month there was definitely a stretch where I felt really charged, like unusually upbeat and convinced my ideas were brilliant. Which is ridiculous because there’s a salad place right downstairs, and I keep buying those little containers of soup thinking I’ll be organized.\n\nBut yeah, my skirts are fitting a bit looser lately. I’m not trying to lose weight, it’s just… food hasn’t been something I’m really paying attention to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your low mood is interfering with your ability to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not completely. I’m still going to work, still getting the basic stuff done at home. And at home I’ve been less dependable with plans—like I’ll say we should do something as a family and then I kind of fade out of it or forget to follow through.\n\nAnd I haven’t really been doing my art things much lately, which is… that’s usually how I stay connected with people. I keep meaning to go, and then I just don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes. Not like I’m physically dragging my feet around the house exactly, but at work people have noticed I’m… slower, I guess.\n\nIn meetings, someone will ask me something, and there’s this pause before I can get the thought together and actually say it. One of my staff sort of joked about it—well, not joked meanly, just noticed—and then I started noticing it too. Like my words are stuck in traffic.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because meetings already have enough weird pauses, and then everyone starts looking at their notebooks or pretending to check the agenda. But yes, I think I am slower to respond than usual.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're moving more slowly physically, not just thinking more slowly?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 46 / manager / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Psychomotor_Changes\nmanifestation: \"My staff has noticed I’m slower to answer questions in meetings, like there’s a pause before I can get my thoughts and words moving.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure it’s my body exactly. I don’t feel like I’m shuffling around or anything dramatic like that. It’s more… in meetings, when someone asks me a question, there’s this lag before I answer.

My staff has noticed it too, which is the part that makes me self-conscious. Like there’s a pause before I can get my thoughts and the words moving. Physically I guess my mouth is slower, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I’m moving slower walking around.

Now I’ll stand there and notice the dishes or some random thing on the counter and it all just feels… flat. It’s a little hard to pin down because at first I was just sort of unusually energized and really driven—like I was pushing through things and taking on more—and then it shifted into this low, heavy feeling that’s been sticking around. But the whole swing, from that revved-up phase to now feeling persistently down, has been around a month.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really… I keep trying to think of one, but the things I usually look forward to just don’t land the same. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Restless or fidgety? Not really in the sense of tapping my foot or pacing around the room. I mean, I do adjust my papers a lot in meetings, but that’s probably just because meetings are awkward and everyone has too many agendas and not enough coffee.

What I’ve noticed more is the opposite, kind of. My staff has noticed I’m slower to answer questions in meetings—like someone asks me something and there’s this pause before I can get my thoughts and words moving. It’s… uncomfortable, because I can feel everyone waiting.

But lately even a regular workday and just getting through dinner at home feels heavier than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, such as hobbies or spending time with friends and family, as much as you did before feeling this exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not as much. I mean, I usually really love painting—like I’ll get all my brushes out and lose a whole afternoon without even noticing. And I like going to museum exhibits too, just kind of wandering and looking at things, even the little description plaques, which my family makes fun of me for reading every word.\n\nBut lately my paints are just sitting there. I see them and think, “I should,” and then I just… don’t. And there was an exhibit I would normally be excited about, but I couldn’t make myself care enough to go. It feels weird, because those are very “me” things.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since you started noticing this exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days lately I wake up with this kind of heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it’s even started. It’s not always dramatic crying or anything—sometimes I still get up and do what I’m supposed to do—but it’s just there.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be more of a morning person, or at least I’d make coffee and look at the light in the kitchen and think about what needed doing. Now I’ll stand there and notice the dishes or some random thing on the counter and it all just feels… flat. Like the whole day is already weighted down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It’s a little hard to pin down because at first I was just sort of unusually energized and really driven—like I was pushing through things and taking on more—and then it shifted into this low, heavy feeling that’s been sticking around. But the whole swing, from that revved-up phase to now feeling persistently down, has been around a month.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really… I keep trying to think of one, but the things I usually look forward to just don’t land the same. Painting is the big one—I normally love setting everything out, mixing colors, making a mess of the table. Lately the supplies are just sitting there, and I keep walking past them like, “Maybe later,” and then later never happens.\n\nAnd museums, too. There was this exhibit I probably would’ve dragged someone to before—or gone by myself, honestly, because I like taking my time. But I just couldn’t make myself care enough to go. It’s strange, because those are usually the things that make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in almost all activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Lately I can’t make myself care enough to go, which is odd because normally that’s the kind of thing I’d plan my whole afternoon around.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of elevated mood or increased energy during the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah… earlier in the month there was definitely a stretch where I felt really charged, like unusually upbeat and convinced my ideas were brilliant. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… not really like I used to. I usually love painting—like, I have all my brushes and little jars set up—and I can spend forever wandering around museum exhibits. Or I used to.

Lately the paint stuff just sits there untouched. I’ll look at it and think I should do something, but then I just… don’t care enough to start. And I keep meaning to go to the museum, because there’s an exhibit I would normally be excited about, but I can’t seem to make myself go. It feels weird, because those are usually my things.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is I’m just really tired. Like, exhausted in a way that sleeping doesn’t seem to fix. I keep thinking maybe it’s just work, because being a manager is always a lot—meetings, emails, people needing things, and then I get home and there’s still dinner and normal life stuff. But lately even a regular workday and just getting through dinner at home feels heavier than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, such as hobbies or spending time with friends and family, as much as you did before feeling this exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not as much. I mean, I usually really love painting—like I’ll get all my brushes out and lose a whole afternoon without even noticing. And I like going to museum exhibits too, just kind of wandering and looking at things, even the little description plaques, which my family makes fun of me for reading every word.\n\nBut lately my paints are just sitting there. I see them and think, “I should,” and then I just… don’t. And there was an exhibit I would normally be excited about, but I couldn’t make myself care enough to go. It feels weird, because those are very “me” things.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since you started noticing this exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days lately I wake up with this kind of heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it’s even started. It’s not always dramatic crying or anything—sometimes I still get up and do what I’m supposed to do—but it’s just there.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be more of a morning person, or at least I’d make coffee and look at the light in the kitchen and think about what needed doing. Now I’ll stand there and notice the dishes or some random thing on the counter and it all just feels… flat. Like the whole day is already weighted down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It’s a little hard to pin down because at first I was just sort of unusually energized and really driven—like I was pushing through things and taking on more—and then it shifted into this low, heavy feeling that’s been sticking around. But the whole swing, from that revved-up phase to now feeling persistently down, has been around a month.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really… I keep trying to think of one, but the things I usually look forward to just don’t land the same. Painting is the big one—I normally love setting everything out, mixing colors, making a mess of the table. Lately the supplies are just sitting there, and I keep walking past them like, “Maybe later,” and then later never happens.\n\nAnd museums, too. There was this exhibit I probably would’ve dragged someone to before—or gone by myself, honestly, because I like taking my time. But I just couldn’t make myself care enough to go. It’s strange, because those are usually the things that make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in almost all activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, “almost all” sounds so big, but… the things I usually really love, like painting, I just don’t touch now. The brushes and paints are sitting there, and I keep meaning to start, but I don’t.\n\nAnd museums—I used to love just wandering around an exhibit, even by myself. Lately I can’t make myself care enough to go, which is odd because normally that’s the kind of thing I’d plan my whole afternoon around.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of elevated mood or increased energy during the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah… earlier in the month there was definitely a stretch where I felt really charged, like unusually upbeat and convinced my ideas were brilliant. I remember making all these plans—some of them were probably fine, but I was very sure about them.\n\nAnd then if my husband asked even a normal question, like “Are you sure that’s a good idea?” I’d get irritated really fast. Which is funny because he questions everything, even what brand of paper towels to buy, but this felt different… like I went from feeling great to snapping at him over nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of death or suicide since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been sitting around planning anything, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes my mind goes to weird places when I’m tired, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like whether I remembered to move the laundry, and then I’m annoyed because the laundry smells damp.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed anything clear like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: A little, I think. I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything, but I’ve been sort of picking at meals… or I’ll get busy at work and realize I just never ate lunch. Which is ridiculous because there’s a salad place right downstairs, and I keep buying those little containers of soup thinking I’ll be organized.\n\nBut yeah, my skirts are fitting a bit looser lately. I’m not trying to lose weight, it’s just… food hasn’t been something I’m really paying attention to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your low mood is interfering with your ability to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not completely. I’m still going to work, still getting the basic stuff done at home. It’s not like everything has fallen apart.\n\nBut I am slower making decisions at work, which is awkward because, you know, I’m supposed to be the person people come to for decisions. And at home I’ve been less dependable with plans—like I’ll say we should do something as a family and then I kind of fade out of it or forget to follow through.\n\nAnd I haven’t really been doing my art things much lately, which is… that’s usually how I stay connected with people. I keep meaning to go, and then I just don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes. Not like I’m physically dragging my feet around the house exactly, but at work people have noticed I’m… slower, I guess.\n\nIn meetings, someone will ask me something, and there’s this pause before I can get the thought together and actually say it. One of my staff sort of joked about it—well, not joked meanly, just noticed—and then I started noticing it too. Like my words are stuck in traffic.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because meetings already have enough weird pauses, and then everyone starts looking at their notebooks or pretending to check the agenda. But yes, I think I am slower to respond than usual.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly physically, not just thinking more slowly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s my body exactly. I don’t feel like I’m shuffling around or anything dramatic like that. It’s more… in meetings, when someone asks me a question, there’s this lag before I answer.\n\nMy staff has noticed it too, which is the part that makes me self-conscious. Like there’s a pause before I can get my thoughts and the words moving. Physically I guess my mouth is slower, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I’m moving slower walking around.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your body is moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Not so much my whole body, I don’t think. I’m not, like, walking in slow motion or anything.\n\nIt’s more that in meetings, if someone asks me something, there’s this pause before I answer. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is I’m just really tired. Like, exhausted in a way that sleeping doesn’t seem to fix. I keep thinking maybe it’s just work, because being a manager is always a lot—meetings, emails, people needing things, and then I get home and there’s still dinner and normal life stuff. But lately even a regular workday and just getting through dinner at home feels heavier than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, such as hobbies or spending time with friends and family, as much as you did before feeling this exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not as much. I mean, I usually really love painting—like I’ll get all my brushes out and lose a whole afternoon without even noticing. And I like going to museum exhibits too, just kind of wandering and looking at things, even the little description plaques, which my family makes fun of me for reading every word.\n\nBut lately my paints are just sitting there. I see them and think, “I should,” and then I just… don’t. And there was an exhibit I would normally be excited about, but I couldn’t make myself care enough to go. It feels weird, because those are very “me” things.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since you started noticing this exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days lately I wake up with this kind of heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it’s even started. It’s not always dramatic crying or anything—sometimes I still get up and do what I’m supposed to do—but it’s just there.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be more of a morning person, or at least I’d make coffee and look at the light in the kitchen and think about what needed doing. Now I’ll stand there and notice the dishes or some random thing on the counter and it all just feels… flat. Like the whole day is already weighted down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It’s a little hard to pin down because at first I was just sort of unusually energized and really driven—like I was pushing through things and taking on more—and then it shifted into this low, heavy feeling that’s been sticking around. But the whole swing, from that revved-up phase to now feeling persistently down, has been around a month.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really… I keep trying to think of one, but the things I usually look forward to just don’t land the same. Painting is the big one—I normally love setting everything out, mixing colors, making a mess of the table. Lately the supplies are just sitting there, and I keep walking past them like, “Maybe later,” and then later never happens.\n\nAnd museums, too. There was this exhibit I probably would’ve dragged someone to before—or gone by myself, honestly, because I like taking my time. But I just couldn’t make myself care enough to go. It’s strange, because those are usually the things that make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in almost all activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, “almost all” sounds so big, but… the things I usually really love, like painting, I just don’t touch now. The brushes and paints are sitting there, and I keep meaning to start, but I don’t.\n\nAnd museums—I used to love just wandering around an exhibit, even by myself. Lately I can’t make myself care enough to go, which is odd because normally that’s the kind of thing I’d plan my whole afternoon around.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of elevated mood or increased energy during the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah… earlier in the month there was definitely a stretch where I felt really charged, like unusually upbeat and convinced my ideas were brilliant. I remember making all these plans—some of them were probably fine, but I was very sure about them.\n\nAnd then if my husband asked even a normal question, like “Are you sure that’s a good idea?” I’d get irritated really fast. Which is funny because he questions everything, even what brand of paper towels to buy, but this felt different… like I went from feeling great to snapping at him over nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of death or suicide since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been sitting around planning anything, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes my mind goes to weird places when I’m tired, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like whether I remembered to move the laundry, and then I’m annoyed because the laundry smells damp.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed anything clear like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: A little, I think. I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything, but I’ve been sort of picking at meals… or I’ll get busy at work and realize I just never ate lunch. Which is ridiculous because there’s a salad place right downstairs, and I keep buying those little containers of soup thinking I’ll be organized.\n\nBut yeah, my skirts are fitting a bit looser lately. I’m not trying to lose weight, it’s just… food hasn’t been something I’m really paying attention to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your low mood is interfering with your ability to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not completely. I’m still going to work, still getting the basic stuff done at home. It’s not like everything has fallen apart.\n\nBut I am slower making decisions at work, which is awkward because, you know, I’m supposed to be the person people come to for decisions. And at home I’ve been less dependable with plans—like I’ll say we should do something as a family and then I kind of fade out of it or forget to follow through.\n\nAnd I haven’t really been doing my art things much lately, which is… that’s usually how I stay connected with people. I keep meaning to go, and then I just don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes. Not like I’m physically dragging my feet around the house exactly, but at work people have noticed I’m… slower, I guess.\n\nIn meetings, someone will ask me something, and there’s this pause before I can get the thought together and actually say it. One of my staff sort of joked about it—well, not joked meanly, just noticed—and then I started noticing it too. Like my words are stuck in traffic.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because meetings already have enough weird pauses, and then everyone starts looking at their notebooks or pretending to check the agenda. But yes, I think I am slower to respond than usual.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly physically, not just thinking more slowly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s my body exactly. I don’t feel like I’m shuffling around or anything dramatic like that. It’s more… in meetings, when someone asks me a question, there’s this lag before I answer.\n\nMy staff has noticed it too, which is the part that makes me self-conscious. Like there’s a pause before I can get my thoughts and the words moving. Physically I guess my mouth is slower, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I’m moving slower walking around.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your body is moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Not so much my whole body, I don’t think. I’m not, like, walking in slow motion or anything.\n\nIt’s more that in meetings, if someone asks me something, there’s this pause before I answer. Which is funny because he questions everything, even what brand of paper towels to buy, but this felt different… like I went from feeling great to snapping at him over nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of death or suicide since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been sitting around planning anything, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes my mind goes to weird places when I’m tired, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like whether I remembered to move the laundry, and then I’m annoyed because the laundry smells damp.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed anything clear like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: A little, I think. I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything, but I’ve been sort of picking at meals… or I’ll get busy at work and realize I just never ate lunch. Which is ridiculous because there’s a salad place right downstairs, and I keep buying those little containers of soup thinking I’ll be organized.\n\nBut yeah, my skirts are fitting a bit looser lately. I’m not trying to lose weight, it’s just… food hasn’t been something I’m really paying attention to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your low mood is interfering with your ability to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not completely. I’m still going to work, still getting the basic stuff done at home. It’s not like everything has fallen apart.\n\nBut I am slower making decisions at work, which is awkward because, you know, I’m supposed to be the person people come to for decisions. And at home I’ve been less dependable with plans—like I’ll say we should do something as a family and then I kind of fade out of it or forget to follow through.\n\nAnd I haven’t really been doing my art things much lately, which is… that’s usually how I stay connected with people. I keep meaning to go, and then I just don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes. Not like I’m physically dragging my feet around the house exactly, but at work people have noticed I’m… slower, I guess.\n\nIn meetings, someone will ask me something, and there’s this pause before I can get the thought together and actually say it. One of my staff sort of joked about it—well, not joked meanly, just noticed—and then I started noticing it too. Like my words are stuck in traffic.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because meetings already have enough weird pauses, and then everyone starts looking at their notebooks or pretending to check the agenda. But yes, I think I am slower to respond than usual.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly physically, not just thinking more slowly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s my body exactly. I don’t feel like I’m shuffling around or anything dramatic like that. It’s more… in meetings, when someone asks me a question, there’s this lag before I answer.\n\nMy staff has noticed it too, which is the part that makes me self-conscious. Like there’s a pause before I can get my thoughts and the words moving. Physically I guess my mouth is slower, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I’m moving slower walking around.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your body is moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Not so much my whole body, I don’t think. I’m not, like, walking in slow motion or anything.\n\nIt’s more that in meetings, if someone asks me something, there’s this pause before I answer. My staff has noticed it too, which is… not great when you’re supposed to be the manager. My staff has noticed I’m slower to answer questions in meetings—like someone asks me something and there’s this pause before I can get my thoughts and words moving. It’s a little hard to pin down because at first I was just sort of unusually energized and really driven—like I was pushing through things and taking on more—and then it shifted into this low, heavy feeling that’s been sticking around. But the whole swing, from that revved-up phase to now feeling persistently down, has been around a month.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really… I keep trying to think of one, but the things I usually look forward to just don’t land the same. Painting is the big one—I normally love setting everything out, mixing colors, making a mess of the table. Lately the supplies are just sitting there, and I keep walking past them like, “Maybe later,” and then later never happens.\n\nAnd museums, too. There was this exhibit I probably would’ve dragged someone to before—or gone by myself, honestly, because I like taking my time. But I just couldn’t make myself care enough to go. It’s strange, because those are usually the things that make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in almost all activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, “almost all” sounds so big, but… the things I usually really love, like painting, I just don’t touch now. The brushes and paints are sitting there, and I keep meaning to start, but I don’t.\n\nAnd museums—I used to love just wandering around an exhibit, even by myself. Lately I can’t make myself care enough to go, which is odd because normally that’s the kind of thing I’d plan my whole afternoon around.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of elevated mood or increased energy during the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah… earlier in the month there was definitely a stretch where I felt really charged, like unusually upbeat and convinced my ideas were brilliant. I remember making all these plans—some of them were probably fine, but I was very sure about them.\n\nAnd then if my husband asked even a normal question, like “Are you sure that’s a good idea?” I’d get irritated really fast. Which is funny because he questions everything, even what brand of paper towels to buy, but this felt different… like I went from feeling great to snapping at him over nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of death or suicide since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been sitting around planning anything, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes my mind goes to weird places when I’m tired, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like whether I remembered to move the laundry, and then I’m annoyed because the laundry smells damp.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed anything clear like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: A little, I think. I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything, but I’ve been sort of picking at meals… or I’ll get busy at work and realize I just never ate lunch. Which is ridiculous because there’s a salad place right downstairs, and I keep buying those little containers of soup thinking I’ll be organized.\n\nBut yeah, my skirts are fitting a bit looser lately. I’m not trying to lose weight, it’s just… food hasn’t been something I’m really paying attention to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your low mood is interfering with your ability to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not completely. I’m still going to work, still getting the basic stuff done at home. It’s not like everything has fallen apart.\n\nBut I am slower making decisions at work, which is awkward because, you know, I’m supposed to be the person people come to for decisions. And at home I’ve been less dependable with plans—like I’ll say we should do something as a family and then I kind of fade out of it or forget to follow through.\n\nAnd I haven’t really been doing my art things much lately, which is… that’s usually how I stay connected with people. I keep meaning to go, and then I just don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes. Not like I’m physically dragging my feet around the house exactly, but at work people have noticed I’m… slower, I guess.\n\nIn meetings, someone will ask me something, and there’s this pause before I can get the thought together and actually say it. One of my staff sort of joked about it—well, not joked meanly, just noticed—and then I started noticing it too. Like my words are stuck in traffic.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because meetings already have enough weird pauses, and then everyone starts looking at their notebooks or pretending to check the agenda. But yes, I think I am slower to respond than usual.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly physically, not just thinking more slowly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s my body exactly. I don’t feel like I’m shuffling around or anything dramatic like that. It’s more… in meetings, when someone asks me a question, there’s this lag before I answer.\n\nMy staff has noticed it too, which is the part that makes me self-conscious. Like there’s a pause before I can get my thoughts and the words moving. Physically I guess my mouth is slower, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I’m moving slower walking around.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your body is moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Not so much my whole body, I don’t think. I’m not, like, walking in slow motion or anything.\n\nIt’s more that in meetings, if someone asks me something, there’s this pause before I answer. My staff has noticed it too, which is… not great when you’re supposed to be the manager. It’s like I can’t get my thoughts and words lined up fast enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Restless or fidgety? Not really in the sense of tapping my foot or pacing around the room. I mean, I do adjust my papers a lot in meetings, but that’s probably just because meetings are awkward and everyone has too many agendas and not enough coffee.\n\nWhat I’ve noticed more is the opposite, kind of. My staff has noticed I’m slower to answer questions in meetings—like someone asks me something and there’s this pause before I can get my thoughts and words moving. It’s… uncomfortable, because I can feel everyone waiting.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really like I used to. I usually love painting—like, I have all my brushes and little jars set up—and I can spend forever wandering around museum exhibits. Or I used to.\n\nLately the paint stuff just sits there untouched. I’ll look at it and think I should do something, but then I just… don’t care enough to start. And I keep meaning to go to the museum, because there’s an exhibit I would normally be excited about, but I can’t seem to make myself go. It feels weird, because those are usually my things.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is mild, moderate, or severe most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’d probably say mild most of the time… though it doesn’t feel like nothing, if that makes sense.\n\nMost days lately I wake up with this heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it even starts. It’s not like I’m completely unable to function, but it’s there in the background. Mornings are the worst for it, I think—well, mornings have never been my favorite, but this is different. It’s just kind of a gray start to everything.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your low mood as being consistently mild or are there times when it feels more moderate to severe?\n\nPatient: I think it stays more on the mild side, honestly. It’s not usually this dramatic thing where I can’t get out of bed or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… most mornings lately I wake up and there’s this heavy, gray feeling already there, like I’m disappointed in the day before anything’s even happened. Which sounds a little ridiculous when I say it out loud—like the day hasn’t even had a chance to be annoying yet, you know? But that’s how it feels. Not severe, just… there, most days.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are any days when your low mood feels worse than mild?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I’d still call it mild overall. It’s more that most days lately I wake up with that heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it’s even started.\n\nAnd then sometimes, if I’ve got a full workday ahead or even just errands—honestly, errands can feel more annoying than work sometimes—it kind of colors everything. But I don’t think it usually tips into something really severe. It’s just… there, heavier some mornings than others.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It’s not like I’m completely unable to function, but it’s there in the background. Mornings are the worst for it, I think—well, mornings have never been my favorite, but this is different. It’s just kind of a gray start to everything.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your low mood as being consistently mild or are there times when it feels more moderate to severe?\n\nPatient: I think it stays more on the mild side, honestly. It’s not usually this dramatic thing where I can’t get out of bed or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… most mornings lately I wake up and there’s this heavy, gray feeling already there, like I’m disappointed in the day before anything’s even happened. Which sounds a little ridiculous when I say it out loud—like the day hasn’t even had a chance to be annoying yet, you know? But that’s how it feels. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… maybe a little more some days, but I’d still put it in the mild category most of the time.

It’s more that lately, a lot of mornings I wake up with this heavy, gray feeling already there, like I’m disappointed in the day before it’s even started. Which is strange, because nothing has happened yet—unless you count my alarm, which honestly is offensive enough on its own. But no, I don’t think it usually becomes really severe. It’s just… heavier some mornings.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is I’m just really tired. Like, exhausted in a way that sleeping doesn’t seem to fix. I keep thinking maybe it’s just work, because being a manager is always a lot—meetings, emails, people needing things, and then I get home and there’s still dinner and normal life stuff. But lately even a regular workday and just getting through dinner at home feels heavier than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, such as hobbies or spending time with friends and family, as much as you did before feeling this exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not as much. I mean, I usually really love painting—like I’ll get all my brushes out and lose a whole afternoon without even noticing. And I like going to museum exhibits too, just kind of wandering and looking at things, even the little description plaques, which my family makes fun of me for reading every word.\n\nBut lately my paints are just sitting there. I see them and think, “I should,” and then I just… don’t. And there was an exhibit I would normally be excited about, but I couldn’t make myself care enough to go. It feels weird, because those are very “me” things.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since you started noticing this exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days lately I wake up with this kind of heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it’s even started. It’s not always dramatic crying or anything—sometimes I still get up and do what I’m supposed to do—but it’s just there.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be more of a morning person, or at least I’d make coffee and look at the light in the kitchen and think about what needed doing. Now I’ll stand there and notice the dishes or some random thing on the counter and it all just feels… flat. Like the whole day is already weighted down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It’s a little hard to pin down because at first I was just sort of unusually energized and really driven—like I was pushing through things and taking on more—and then it shifted into this low, heavy feeling that’s been sticking around. But the whole swing, from that revved-up phase to now feeling persistently down, has been around a month.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really… I keep trying to think of one, but the things I usually look forward to just don’t land the same. Painting is the big one—I normally love setting everything out, mixing colors, making a mess of the table. Lately the supplies are just sitting there, and I keep walking past them like, “Maybe later,” and then later never happens.\n\nAnd museums, too. There was this exhibit I probably would’ve dragged someone to before—or gone by myself, honestly, because I like taking my time. But I just couldn’t make myself care enough to go. It’s strange, because those are usually the things that make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in almost all activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, “almost all” sounds so big, but… the things I usually really love, like painting, I just don’t touch now. The brushes and paints are sitting there, and I keep meaning to start, but I don’t.\n\nAnd museums—I used to love just wandering around an exhibit, even by myself. Lately I can’t make myself care enough to go, which is odd because normally that’s the kind of thing I’d plan my whole afternoon around.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of elevated mood or increased energy during the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah… earlier in the month there was definitely a stretch where I felt really charged, like unusually upbeat and convinced my ideas were brilliant. I remember making all these plans—some of them were probably fine, but I was very sure about them.\n\nAnd then if my husband asked even a normal question, like “Are you sure that’s a good idea?” I’d get irritated really fast. Which is funny because he questions everything, even what brand of paper towels to buy, but this felt different… like I went from feeling great to snapping at him over nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of death or suicide since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been sitting around planning anything, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes my mind goes to weird places when I’m tired, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like whether I remembered to move the laundry, and then I’m annoyed because the laundry smells damp.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed anything clear like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: A little, I think. I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything, but I’ve been sort of picking at meals… or I’ll get busy at work and realize I just never ate lunch. Which is ridiculous because there’s a salad place right downstairs, and I keep buying those little containers of soup thinking I’ll be organized.\n\nBut yeah, my skirts are fitting a bit looser lately. I’m not trying to lose weight, it’s just… food hasn’t been something I’m really paying attention to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your low mood is interfering with your ability to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not completely. I’m still going to work, still getting the basic stuff done at home. It’s not like everything has fallen apart.\n\nBut I am slower making decisions at work, which is awkward because, you know, I’m supposed to be the person people come to for decisions. And at home I’ve been less dependable with plans—like I’ll say we should do something as a family and then I kind of fade out of it or forget to follow through.\n\nAnd I haven’t really been doing my art things much lately, which is… that’s usually how I stay connected with people. I keep meaning to go, and then I just don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes. Not like I’m physically dragging my feet around the house exactly, but at work people have noticed I’m… slower, I guess.\n\nIn meetings, someone will ask me something, and there’s this pause before I can get the thought together and actually say it. One of my staff sort of joked about it—well, not joked meanly, just noticed—and then I started noticing it too. Like my words are stuck in traffic.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because meetings already have enough weird pauses, and then everyone starts looking at their notebooks or pretending to check the agenda. But yes, I think I am slower to respond than usual.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly physically, not just thinking more slowly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s my body exactly. I don’t feel like I’m shuffling around or anything dramatic like that. It’s more… in meetings, when someone asks me a question, there’s this lag before I answer.\n\nMy staff has noticed it too, which is the part that makes me self-conscious. Like there’s a pause before I can get my thoughts and the words moving. Physically I guess my mouth is slower, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I’m moving slower walking around.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your body is moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Not so much my whole body, I don’t think. I’m not, like, walking in slow motion or anything.\n\nIt’s more that in meetings, if someone asks me something, there’s this pause before I answer. My staff has noticed it too, which is… not great when you’re supposed to be the manager. It’s like I can’t get my thoughts and words lined up fast enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Restless or fidgety? Not really in the sense of tapping my foot or pacing around the room. I mean, I do adjust my papers a lot in meetings, but that’s probably just because meetings are awkward and everyone has too many agendas and not enough coffee.\n\nWhat I’ve noticed more is the opposite, kind of. My staff has noticed I’m slower to answer questions in meetings—like someone asks me something and there’s this pause before I can get my thoughts and words moving. It’s… uncomfortable, because I can feel everyone waiting.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really like I used to. I usually love painting—like, I have all my brushes and little jars set up—and I can spend forever wandering around museum exhibits. Or I used to.\n\nLately the paint stuff just sits there untouched. I’ll look at it and think I should do something, but then I just… don’t care enough to start. And I keep meaning to go to the museum, because there’s an exhibit I would normally be excited about, but I can’t seem to make myself go. It feels weird, because those are usually my things.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is mild, moderate, or severe most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’d probably say mild most of the time… though it doesn’t feel like nothing, if that makes sense.\n\nMost days lately I wake up with this heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it even starts. It’s not like I’m completely unable to function, but it’s there in the background. Mornings are the worst for it, I think—well, mornings have never been my favorite, but this is different. It’s just kind of a gray start to everything.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your low mood as being consistently mild or are there times when it feels more moderate to severe?\n\nPatient: I think it stays more on the mild side, honestly. It’s not usually this dramatic thing where I can’t get out of bed or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… most mornings lately I wake up and there’s this heavy, gray feeling already there, like I’m disappointed in the day before anything’s even happened. Not severe, just… there, most days.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are any days when your low mood feels worse than mild?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I’d still call it mild overall. I’m still going to work, still getting the basic stuff done at home. It’s not like everything has fallen apart.\n\nBut I am slower making decisions at work, which is awkward because, you know, I’m supposed to be the person people come to for decisions. And at home I’ve been less dependable with plans—like I’ll say we should do something as a family and then I kind of fade out of it or forget to follow through.\n\nAnd I haven’t really been doing my art things much lately, which is… that’s usually how I stay connected with people. I keep meaning to go, and then I just don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes. Not like I’m physically dragging my feet around the house exactly, but at work people have noticed I’m… slower, I guess.\n\nIn meetings, someone will ask me something, and there’s this pause before I can get the thought together and actually say it. One of my staff sort of joked about it—well, not joked meanly, just noticed—and then I started noticing it too. Like my words are stuck in traffic.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because meetings already have enough weird pauses, and then everyone starts looking at their notebooks or pretending to check the agenda. But yes, I think I am slower to respond than usual.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly physically, not just thinking more slowly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s my body exactly. I don’t feel like I’m shuffling around or anything dramatic like that. It’s more… in meetings, when someone asks me a question, there’s this lag before I answer.\n\nMy staff has noticed it too, which is the part that makes me self-conscious. I’m not, like, walking in slow motion or anything.\n\nIt’s more that in meetings, if someone asks me something, there’s this pause before I answer. Most days lately I wake up with this kind of heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it’s even started. It’s not always dramatic crying or anything—sometimes I still get up and do what I’m supposed to do—but it’s just there.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be more of a morning person, or at least I’d make coffee and look at the light in the kitchen and think about what needed doing. Now I’ll stand there and notice the dishes or some random thing on the counter and it all just feels… flat. Like the whole day is already weighted down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It’s a little hard to pin down because at first I was just sort of unusually energized and really driven—like I was pushing through things and taking on more—and then it shifted into this low, heavy feeling that’s been sticking around. But the whole swing, from that revved-up phase to now feeling persistently down, has been around a month.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not really… I keep trying to think of one, but the things I usually look forward to just don’t land the same. Painting is the big one—I normally love setting everything out, mixing colors, making a mess of the table. Lately the supplies are just sitting there, and I keep walking past them like, “Maybe later,” and then later never happens.\n\nAnd museums, too. There was this exhibit I probably would’ve dragged someone to before—or gone by myself, honestly, because I like taking my time. But I just couldn’t make myself care enough to go. It’s strange, because those are usually the things that make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in almost all activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, “almost all” sounds so big, but… the things I usually really love, like painting, I just don’t touch now. The brushes and paints are sitting there, and I keep meaning to start, but I don’t.\n\nAnd museums—I used to love just wandering around an exhibit, even by myself. Lately I can’t make myself care enough to go, which is odd because normally that’s the kind of thing I’d plan my whole afternoon around.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of elevated mood or increased energy during the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah… earlier in the month there was definitely a stretch where I felt really charged, like unusually upbeat and convinced my ideas were brilliant. I remember making all these plans—some of them were probably fine, but I was very sure about them.\n\nAnd then if my husband asked even a normal question, like “Are you sure that’s a good idea?” I’d get irritated really fast. Which is funny because he questions everything, even what brand of paper towels to buy, but this felt different… like I went from feeling great to snapping at him over nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of death or suicide since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been sitting around planning anything, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes my mind goes to weird places when I’m tired, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like whether I remembered to move the laundry, and then I’m annoyed because the laundry smells damp.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed anything clear like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: A little, I think. I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything, but I’ve been sort of picking at meals… or I’ll get busy at work and realize I just never ate lunch. Which is ridiculous because there’s a salad place right downstairs, and I keep buying those little containers of soup thinking I’ll be organized.\n\nBut yeah, my skirts are fitting a bit looser lately. I’m not trying to lose weight, it’s just… food hasn’t been something I’m really paying attention to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your low mood is interfering with your ability to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not completely. I’m still going to work, still getting the basic stuff done at home. It’s not like everything has fallen apart.\n\nBut I am slower making decisions at work, which is awkward because, you know, I’m supposed to be the person people come to for decisions. And at home I’ve been less dependable with plans—like I’ll say we should do something as a family and then I kind of fade out of it or forget to follow through.\n\nAnd I haven’t really been doing my art things much lately, which is… that’s usually how I stay connected with people. I keep meaning to go, and then I just don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yes. Not like I’m physically dragging my feet around the house exactly, but at work people have noticed I’m… slower, I guess.\n\nIn meetings, someone will ask me something, and there’s this pause before I can get the thought together and actually say it. One of my staff sort of joked about it—well, not joked meanly, just noticed—and then I started noticing it too. Like my words are stuck in traffic.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because meetings already have enough weird pauses, and then everyone starts looking at their notebooks or pretending to check the agenda. But yes, I think I am slower to respond than usual.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly physically, not just thinking more slowly?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s my body exactly. I don’t feel like I’m shuffling around or anything dramatic like that. It’s more… in meetings, when someone asks me a question, there’s this lag before I answer.\n\nMy staff has noticed it too, which is the part that makes me self-conscious. Like there’s a pause before I can get my thoughts and the words moving. Physically I guess my mouth is slower, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I’m moving slower walking around.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your body is moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Not so much my whole body, I don’t think. I’m not, like, walking in slow motion or anything.\n\nIt’s more that in meetings, if someone asks me something, there’s this pause before I answer. My staff has noticed it too, which is… not great when you’re supposed to be the manager. It’s like I can’t get my thoughts and words lined up fast enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Restless or fidgety? Not really in the sense of tapping my foot or pacing around the room. I mean, I do adjust my papers a lot in meetings, but that’s probably just because meetings are awkward and everyone has too many agendas and not enough coffee.\n\nWhat I’ve noticed more is the opposite, kind of. My staff has noticed I’m slower to answer questions in meetings—like someone asks me something and there’s this pause before I can get my thoughts and words moving. It’s… uncomfortable, because I can feel everyone waiting.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really like I used to. I usually love painting—like, I have all my brushes and little jars set up—and I can spend forever wandering around museum exhibits. Or I used to.\n\nLately the paint stuff just sits there untouched. I’ll look at it and think I should do something, but then I just… don’t care enough to start. And I keep meaning to go to the museum, because there’s an exhibit I would normally be excited about, but I can’t seem to make myself go. It feels weird, because those are usually my things.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your low mood is mild, moderate, or severe most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’d probably say mild most of the time… though it doesn’t feel like nothing, if that makes sense.\n\nMost days lately I wake up with this heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it even starts. It’s not like I’m completely unable to function, but it’s there in the background. Mornings are the worst for it, I think—well, mornings have never been my favorite, but this is different. It’s just kind of a gray start to everything.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your low mood as being consistently mild or are there times when it feels more moderate to severe?\n\nPatient: I think it stays more on the mild side, honestly. It’s not usually this dramatic thing where I can’t get out of bed or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… most mornings lately I wake up and there’s this heavy, gray feeling already there, like I’m disappointed in the day before anything’s even happened. Which sounds a little ridiculous when I say it out loud—like the day hasn’t even had a chance to be annoying yet, you know? But that’s how it feels. Not severe, just… there, most days.\n\nDoctor: Would you say there are any days when your low mood feels worse than mild?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I’d still call it mild overall. It’s more that most days lately I wake up with that heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it’s even started.\n\nAnd then sometimes, if I’ve got a full workday ahead or even just errands—honestly, errands can feel more annoying than work sometimes—it kind of colors everything. But I don’t think it usually tips into something really severe. It’s just… there, heavier some mornings than others.\n\nDoctor: Are there any days when you feel like your low mood is more than just mild?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little more some days, but I’d still put it in the mild category most of the time.\n\nIt’s more that lately, a lot of mornings I wake up with this heavy, gray feeling already there, like I’m disappointed in the day before it’s even started. Which is strange, because nothing has happened yet—unless you count my alarm, which honestly is offensive enough on its own. But no, I don’t think it usually becomes really severe. It’s just… heavier some mornings.\n\nDoctor: Are there any days when your low mood feels more than just mild?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, some mornings it feels a little heavier, but I still don’t know that I’d call it more than mild. It’s more that lately I wake up most days with this heavy, gray feeling already sitting there, like I’m disappointed in the day before it even starts.\n\nAnd then I’ll be making coffee or staring at my closet trying to decide what to wear, which somehow becomes this whole unnecessary ordeal, and I’m like, why am I already tired of today? But it’s not usually… I don’t know, extreme. Just there."
